**[SRC brief.md · UPDATE PASS: every brief entry newer than the first build, "Youngstown reaches out" through "Sophomore housing" (end of "Player directives") — NEWEST; NEWER WINS · L1552-L1658]**

- **Youngstown reaches out (player; S2, January on).** Once Jack and Skye are public on Facebook and in the tabloids,
  **a lot of old friends from school reach out** (the New Year's Eve crowd at Ryan Pallante's and beyond): wall posts,
  messages, friend requests from people he hasn't spoken to since St. Brigid's/high school, the CYO guys, Tommy Hritz,
  exes of friends, someone's mom. Comic texture (period-true Facebook insert material) with an edge: everyone back
  home suddenly sees him, and none of them ask about the girl in New Orleans. Gina fields some of it at school.
- **S2E03 design answers (player).**
  - **The reunion: at the gate, loudly.** Skye is waiting at the airport (sign, sunglasses at night, the works) and
    kisses him like nobody's watching; **someone with a phone is**. That photo becomes the first fan/gossip shot of them.
  - **Lena on the first day back: she's just gone.** She's seriously upset and doesn't want to be seen by anybody.
    **When they're forced next to each other, it's perfect politeness, which quickly unravels into the first sideways
    fight** (the proxy war opens; the Facebook-status grievance itself is saved for a later fight per the brief).
- **S2E03 Friday call refinement (player; patches outlines/s2e03.md s09).** The plan's hint stands ("I'm going to see
  him Monday… I haven't been fair to him… I'm going to fix it."), plus the player's addition: **she's going to coffee
  or dinner with Julian and asks if Jack will be around after.** Jack hears "seeing Julian" and takes the rest as a
  friend's courtesy or a back-off signal, and his answer is evasive or a "probably not / I've got a thing" (he'll be
  with Skye): **on a replay, she was asking him to be there right after she ended it.** The call ends awkwardly as
  planned (he hangs up first). Written both ways; her intent off-screen.
  - **Lena's read of the awkward call (player; important).** She must **still believe they understand each other until
    she sees the status.** So she takes his "probably not / I've got a thing" and the abrupt ending as **weird, or as
    him working a shift / being tired / something practical** (Jack hears her accept it easily: "Okay. Then call me
    after." or similar), **not as a rejection.** Her decision stands; nothing on the call shakes it. Only the Facebook
    status breaks it. (Write her side of the call so a replay shows her unbothered certainty; Jack, on first hearing,
    reads her easy acceptance as more proof she's moving on.)
- **The airport reunion video reaches Jack's wall (player).** Within a day or two, **somebody tags him in or posts the
  airport reunion video on his wall** (period: an iPhone 3GS or Flip video uploaded to Facebook or YouTube; a fan, a
  gossip blog, or a Youngstown friend reposting it). **Lena sees it as well** (off-screen per POV; it's part of what
  Laney finds going through the pictures and interactions after taking the laptop). Jack learns it's out there from
  the wall/comments and Gina, and only learns in New Orleans that Lena watched it.
- **Skye's first defiant posts (player; refines "she goes official herself" and the reckless posting).** If there's a
  clear still from the airport video (or a good photo of the moment from another phone), **Skye puts it on Facebook
  herself.** When **her mom/team tries to get her to take it down**, that's when **she takes the first photo of the two
  of them herself and posts it instead**: the start of the reckless posting, born of defiance.
- **The Saab singalong video (player; a little later, late Jan/early Feb).** Someone in traffic films them in the Saab,
  **top down, at a stoplight, singing a period-accurate and absolutely filthy song to each other at the top of their
  lungs, eyes on each other the whole time.** It goes around (wall posts, a gossip blog), and the friends, Gina, and
  Lena all see it. Pure joy on the screen (the double state's Skye half at full volume). **Song: room's pick, released
  by the scene's date**; candidates: Khia, "My Neck, My Back (Lick It)" (2002); Jeremih, "Birthday Sex" (2009);
  Lady Gaga, "LoveGame" (2008); Young Money, "BedRock" (Nov 2009). Quote at most a few words of lyric on the page.
  - **Song locked (player): Jeremih, "Birthday Sex"** (2009). Quote at most a few words of lyric on the page.
- **Birthdays get celebrated (player; standing).** Every main character's birthday is marked on-screen when it comes
  up on the calendar (a scene, a beat, a text, a card: scaled to the person and the moment). Bible §9 is the
  calendar. **Showrunner: fill the gaps in §9 at the next planning pass** (Frank, Maria, Gina, Rosie's exact date,
  Nonna, Danny confirmed Feb 2; the Broussards: Tony, Celeste, Mémère, Tess, Drew, Lena's other siblings; Sol; Fr. Dom
  if recurring), and check each S2 outline against the calendar. **S2 birthdays already on the calendar:**
  - **Julian, Jan 9 (S2E03)**: the day Jack lands and Skye meets him at the gate. It's also why Lena's "I'm going to see
    him Monday" isn't the weekend: **she won't end it on his birthday.** (Room: honor this in S2E03; Lena's off-screen
    weekend is Julian's birthday.)
  - **Danny, Feb 2** (Candlemas; 23; his letter). **Evan, Feb 11** (they're away). **Laney, Feb 14** (Valentine's,
    Bacchus Sunday, in New Orleans; Celeste's cake). **Kevin, Feb 15** (Presidents' Day, in New Orleans: don't let
    Laney's eclipse it entirely). **Peter Haddad, Mar 8.** **Jack, Mar 19** (St. Joseph; date #2). **Skye, Apr 4
    (Easter Sunday 2010)**, after the breakup: Jack's choice whether to send anything (room's call). **Osei, May 2.**
    **Becca, May 31** (summer; a text). Then Rafi Jun 2, Hale Jun 30, Brooks Jul 4, Lena Jul 22 (S3).
  - **The singalong video is a big deal for both of them (player).** **Jack:** Youngstown sees it (the family, St.
    Brigid's/the NYE crowd, the parish) and so does **the Newman crowd** (Osei, Mrs. Fitch, Becca, the regulars): the
    devout guy belting "Birthday Sex" in a convertible; comic and mortifying, with real fallout (Ma's call; a look from
    Osei; Laney's delight). **Skye:** her **reputation and image as Cassidy**, the network's good girl: the team and her
    mother in full crisis mode, gossip-blog headlines about the "good girl" gone wild. She doesn't regret it; it may
    be the happiest thing she's ever been filmed doing. **It is the public antic that triggers the alliance's third (biggest) emergency meeting** (player-approved), the one that secretly gets Lena to plan the New Orleans trip.
  - **Jack confesses the singalong as what it was (player).** No wrong intended: a fun moment that wasn't thought
    through. **A venial sin**, in the sense that it continues the **regularizing and cheapening of the words**, making
    the act easier to treat lightly, **not just for them but for others who see it** (a small public-example /
    scandal dimension), **but not a reveling in the gravely sinful act itself.** Jack names it precisely, without
    scrupulosity and without excuse. Osei's response fits his current stage of the arc (he may well enjoy it a
    little; real counsel about words and witness, never a scolding).
  - **Jack apologizes to Skye (player; adapt to voice).** Later, Jack apologizes for causing problems for her career.
    **She takes his face in her hands, kisses him slow and hard, then tells him never to apologize for making her
    happy.**
  - **Skye's response to the video (player).** Unlike everything before, **she does NOT share the video herself**,
    solely because she knows how the people around Jack see him in it (his family, Youngstown, the Newman crowd).
    But she **posts on his Facebook wall, deliberately undermining her own team's PR campaign** (whatever spin they're
    running), so it's public that **she's looking forward to their next singalong**, with a wink to **"bring the clean
    version next time, for everybody watching at home."** Protecting him and defying them in one post.
- **Order and turn in Skye's public posting (player; refines "reckless posting").**
  1. Defiant posts escalate (airport still → her own photo → wilder ones) **up to one suggestive photo post.**
  2. **That's where she learns some of it costs him** (his family's reaction: Ma, Nonna; Youngstown; the Newman crowd).
     And by now **she really wants his mom and Nonna to like her.** So **from then on she shares only sweet/happy photos
     and videos.**
  3. **Jack tells her he doesn't care**: he likes being with her, likes having fun with her, and she shouldn't feel bad
     for posting something true. **She can tell he's being truthful and genuinely doesn't care. She stops anyway**,
     because it still costs him, and it costs her with his family.
  4. **The singalong video comes after this** (filmed by a stranger, not posted by her), which is why she doesn't
     share it and answers it with the protective, defiant wall post instead.
- **Summer 2010 (S3) visits (player).** They plan **one long trip each way**: Lena to Youngstown, and Jack to New
  Orleans. (Supersedes shorter "trade visits.")
- **Skye's birthday, Apr 4 (Easter Sunday 2010) (player).** Jack **struggles over whether to text her**: he wants to do
  whatever's best for her on that front (is it kind or cruel to reach out?). Show the struggle; the room decides what
  he does, or makes it a player choice.
- **Jack and Lena's physical intimacy as a couple (player; S2 spring → S3).** It **builds and escalates consistently
  but very slowly.** They touch each other a lot, but **it's not raw and hungry at every moment**; they do other things
  in each other's arms too (talk, read, argue, sleep on a couch, watch the game). **So restraint is not a huge factor
  yet**: a couple of light moments in S2 and S3, **then it becomes the fire from S4 (Fall 2010) on.** (Contrast with
  Skye, which ran hot from the start; Lena's is slower and deeper.)
- **Sophomore housing (player; S4 = Fall 2010).** **Laney lives in her sorority house.** **Lena and Margot become
  roommates in the dorm.** Jack and Theo: see the next entry.
  - **Jack, Theo, Kevin and Yusuf rent a house (player).** Four guys in a beat-up rental (CORRECTED below: Theo pays
    exactly the same share): a porch, a kitchen, house rules, Lena and Margot always over; **the ensemble's new hub
    from S4.** (The spring 2010 lease and deposit happen on-screen in S2E28–E30; room: reconcile with registry #33 rather than overwrite it.)
  - **The hub, standing through graduation (player).** The house stays the ensemble's hub for the rest of school.
    **Laney is over all the time too** (slightly less than the others because of sorority obligations). **Senior
    year:** Jack and Lena move in together as a married couple, and **Margot replaces Jack** as the fourth resident
    (Margot, Theo, Kevin, Yusuf). Purpose: a shared home set makes it easy to weave everybody's individual arcs
    around the central relationship arc.
  - **Theo and the house money (player; corrects "pays more than his share").** **Theo pays exactly the same rent and
    utilities, because the other three insist.** But **he keeps buying a ton of things he "wants for the house,"** so
    they all live very well for college students: the house hosts **lots of house parties, football games, other big
    events, and the communal liturgical celebrations/parties planned by Jack and Lena** (and Kevin, when it fits his
    arc; S3+ liturgical-year directive).


## 1C. Bible standing specs the directives point at (verbatim) — Skye's picture ladder and heat ladder (rung 8 and S2-a are this episode's)


**[SRC bible.md · Picture texts (brief, "Skye's picture texts") — constraints, then rungs 7 and 8 and onward (§10.2). [Packer: rungs 1–6 (E19–S2E01), bible L1313–1315, omitted here] · L1310–1318]**

**Picture texts (brief, "Skye's picture texts"; from S1E19; added by the S1E19 revision-2 pass).** Her iPhone got US picture messaging on Sep 25, 2009 and she's still delighted with the trick; no front
camera until June 2010, so **every picture is a mirror shot with the white 3GS in the frame** (or a blind arm's-length back-camera shot). On Jack's RAZR they arrive as *New Picture Msg*, crawl in over a slow
connection, and show the size of a postage stamp, dim, tilted to a light to be read (≈a quarter each on the Costigan family plan, which Maria reads line by line — Researcher to confirm); they sit in the inbox and
the Pictures folder until erased, on a phone with no real lock. **The ladder escalates and is tracked like the heat scenes** (no two rungs the same):

[… rungs 1–6 (E19 PM1 – S2E01 rung 6) omitted by the Packer; see bible.md L1313–1315 …]

→ **S2E02 rung 7 (S2E02 plan rev. 1), Wed Dec 30, 10:22 a.m. EST (7:22 PST) — escalation by HIS situation:** after the call; apart, consecutive days; **it arrives in Frank's hand** — Frank's phone is dead and he has borrowed the RAZR to call the union hall; "You got a picture." — he hands it back shut and finishes on the kitchen phone (nobody sees anything); opened-and-answered / left shut (`pic7_unopened`) / erased (`pic7_erased`) in the garage (`s2e2_pic`); **the notch: the first taken outdoors, in daylight, and the first with a number on it** — she went out at six to surf "so she'd stop thinking about the phone": a cove up the coast at first light, the wetsuit peeled to her hips, nothing above it, wet hair, gooseflesh, the board's nose in frame; a blind arm's-length back-camera shot (not a mirror); **a 10 drawn in the sand at her feet** (ten days to Jan 9) — or, the Writer's alternative, in surf zinc low at her hip where his thumb stopped on the bluff (E22b) — one or the other; **a new expression: no performance in it at all** (salt-blotched, squinting into the low sun, mouth just open from the cold); captions *10. im not counting* / *went out at 6 so id stop thinking about the phone. didnt work. the ocean says hi*; [`pic6_unopened`] rung 6 may be opened during the call itself (s08, a gated choice) → **S2E03 rung 8 (S2E03 plan rev. 1), Thu Jan 7, 11:16 p.m. EST (8:16 PST) — escalation by her daring and by place:** the first since she came north and **the first taken at Del Playa**, in the room he has been in and will be in again in days (she moved back into Sol's house that afternoon; the heater broken); it arrives in the back bedroom on Glenwood **while he is halfway through his letter telling Danny she isn't blind**; **a new camera action — her own reflection in her bedroom's black window at night** (the room lit behind her, the beach dark beyond, the glass giving her back over the white line of the surf; the 3GS at her eye; not a mirror, not blind, not from above); nothing on; **a new expression — defiance at the dark**, chin up, daring Del Playa and the Pacific to look; on the RAZR at first two of her (the reflection and the girl) until he tilts it to Danny's lamp; captions *moved in. heater broken* / *nobody on the beach. i checked. mostly*; open-and-answer / answer unopened (*my brother wants to know if youre blind* → *tell him 20/15*) / leave shut (`pic8_unopened`) / erase (`pic8_erased`) (`s2e3_pic`); **at SBA (Jan 9) any unopened rungs (7, 8 — rung 6 was opened, S2E01 played) may be opened in the Saab with her watching his face, not the screen** (`pics_opened_with_skye` — the first time she watches him look; each rung named in a clause, never re-described) → 
Jan–Feb → **New Orleans, the most salacious one, right after he sends her to voicemail** (brief; open-or-delete flagged as a possible choice). Within the hard rules and the literary standard: described once each,
specifically, never pornographic, never on a journal card or in a recap. **Jack becomes visibly private with his phone December–February** (tilt, snap shut, pocket, under the pillow) — **Theo and Lena notice**
(and the family at Christmas); nobody but Jack ever sees a picture.


**[SRC bible.md · Heat ladder — the standing rule, then the call and S2-a and onward (§10.2). [Packer: rungs E16–E22b omitted here] · L1319–1319]**

**Heat ladder (tracked; brief "Skye heat-scene escalation" + "Skye heat ladder, standing rule"; added by the S1E22 plan rev. 2).** Every new intimate scene escalates and **names what is new against the last**; each no is harder than the last (resolve, body, argument, timing); **after any no but the first, the heat continues at full intensity, confined to the limit** (to the letter) — never ending or fading on the no. Rungs so far: [… E16–E17 long kissing → E18 words → E19b the turnout → E20a/b the honeywagon and her bed (the first no stops everything) → E21a her room (to the letter) → E22a the Marine Lab turnout (his jeans off) → E22b the bluff (he leads; his own unasked no) — omitted by the Packer; see bible.md L1319 …] → **the Christmas-break call (S2E02 s08–s08c, Tue Dec 29, 1:34–3:41 a.m. EST, the Glenwood basement — his back against the furnace, not the bottom step; it opens on the five-day she was owed and **ends when the RAZR's battery dies mid-goodnight — nobody hangs up** (S2E02 rev. 2); S2E01 held the ladder for it)** — talk only, but **by far the most explicit scene of the arc** (the only time actual sex is described, not happening). **What's new (S2E02 plan):** "Monday. Not asking. **Telling.**" — (1) the act itself described, as future, in her words and then his; (2) he says what he wants *done*, not only that he wants (her gift, "saying it out loud," spent on this); (3) **no "no" at all** — nothing is asked, so nothing is refused; the line holds by distance alone (on [Restraint] he reaches for the no and it isn't there); (4) her drop is about the saying ("I've never said it before. I've only ever done it."). He is not as good at it as she is; it passes; he's shaken → *(S2E03 rev. 1 held the ladder; **superseded by S2E03 rev. 2**, player notes: "heat ladder" is due in E03)* the SBA curb kiss and the Buellton pump kiss stay a greeting and a celebration, **not rungs**; the call is never quoted → **S2-a (S2E03 rev. 2, Mon Jan 11 ≈11 p.m., Del Playa, her room — the broken heater, the black window of rung 8, the lamp on):** the first in-person rung since the call. **What's new:** (1) **the call's words made present** — she asks for one sentence of his from the phone, here, in the light, eyes open (never quoted on the page; [Candor] he says it to her face); (2) **her last layer off, by her own hand, lamp on — the first time he sees all of her**; his stays on, and the line now sits at his last layer; (3) **she asks in the present tense** ("Now." — not "Monday," not "not asking"); (4) **the no is the hardest yet** — none of it is the first time any more (S2E02 s08b's premonition paid: the real yes is easier now); by `s2e3_rung` it comes plainly ([Candor]), late, at the very edge ([Reckless]), or **before she asks** ([Restraint] — "You said it first." — her surprise), or with his arms around her and the reason in one sentence ([Sincerity]); (5) **after the no, to the letter** — she doesn't dress; nothing ends; the heat goes on, confined, until they sleep. Literary; no mechanics; no procedural consent beats. **S2-b (E05) must name its step past all of her, lamp on, and his last layer as the line** → **January–February in person** — each rung past the last, short of sex (HARD RULE): **S2-b (S2E05), S2-c (S2E07), S2-d (S2E09), S2-e (S2E10)** → the near-miss (**S2E11**, re-mapped) → the most salacious picture in New Orleans (**S2E14**, open-or-delete). The Showrunner and Voice Editor name each step in the outline; no two scenes the same beat; proportionate to the episode's heart.

# 2. STYLE


**[SRC bible.md · §3 Tone · L67–75]**

\## 3. Tone

*Warmth first, knife second.* Gilmore Girls in the pace and the banter, Hart of Dixie in the sweetness, Greek in the
houses, Task and Strike in the patience of the mystery, Red Dead in the moral weight of one good bad man, The Last of Us
in the way love is the thing that damns and saves. Euphoria only in honesty about bodies and drugs, never in glamour.

Rule of thumb: **three laughs before every cut.** A dark scene earns its darkness by following warmth. A devastating
episode still has a scene where someone eats pie and is ridiculous.


**[SRC bible.md · §4 Style guide (§4.1 POV and tense · §4.2 Prose register · §4.3 Dialogue rules · §4.4 How heat is written · §4.5 How faith is written · §4.6 How the arguments are written · §4.7 How comedy works · §4.8 Content standards) — IN FULL · L76–227]**

\## 4. Style guide

\### 4.1 POV and tense
- **Close third, present tense, Jack only.** "Jack watches her cross the porch." The camera never leaves Jack. No
  cutaways, no "meanwhile," no dreams that show what Jack cannot know (a dream may show only what Jack fears).
- Narration knows what Jack knows and sees what Jack sees. It may infer ("something in her face says she has done this
  before"); it may never assert another person's thoughts as fact.
- Jack's interiority is allowed and important, but **dramatized**, not essayed. One sentence of thought, then action.
- The narration's intelligence is Jack's intelligence: a well-read nineteen-year-old from the Rust Belt. It can quote
  Chesterton; it should also notice the price of the gas.

\### 4.2 Prose register
- **Literary, warm, funny, sensory. A TV hour on the page.** Scenes are built like television scenes: an establishing
  image (the chyron does the place and time, the first sentence does the light, the weather, the smell), a turn, an exit.
- **Specific nouns.** Not "a drink" — a red Solo cup of Natty Light gone warm. Not "a car" — a 1999 Chevy Silverado with
  rust blooming over the rear wheel wells like a rash. Not "music" — whatever is actually playing.
- **Sensory anchors at Marisol:** eucalyptus (menthol, cat-pee, rain), kelp at low tide, sunscreen, fog that beads on
  wool, the 2 a.m. train horn, sea lions barking off the wharf, jasmine on the Las Olas fences, the sweet chemical smell
  of the Delgado laundry room. **At Youngstown:** wet leaves, the cold metal smell of the garage, Nonna's gravy, Folgers,
  diesel, snow that is gray by noon. **At New Orleans:** sweet olive, boiled crab, rain on hot asphalt, mold, chicory.
- **Sentence music:** vary length. Allow a long, accumulating sentence when Jack is overwhelmed; then a short one.
- **Paragraphs:** dialogue lives inside prose, novel-style. One speaker per paragraph unless it's a rapid-fire exchange
  that reads better stacked.
- **Banned:** the tic registry in `room.md`. Also: "orbs," "padded," "smirked" more than once an episode, "gaze" as a
  verb, "electric" for touch, "a jolt," "time stood still," "the world fell away," "let out a breath he didn't know he
  was holding." No eyes that "darken with desire." No "core."

\### 4.3 Dialogue rules
- **Everyone has their own funny.** See voices in Part II. Jack is wry and allusive with Rust Belt bluntness; Lena is
  fast, Southern, combative, theatrical and suddenly tender; Theo is epigrammatic and luxurious; Laney is pageant-Texan
  with filthy timing; Margot is deadpan and polysyllabic; Kevin is dad-joke sunny with a scalpel under it; Osei is
  proverbial and mischievous; Hale is Oxbridge cut-glass with gutter vocabulary.
- **Subtext over text.** People rarely say what they mean when it matters. They talk about the pie. Nobody says "I'm
  scared." They say "Is it cold in here or is it Ohio."
- **2009 mouths.** No therapy-speak ("boundaries," "triggered," "valid," "unpack," "toxic," "lived experience") except
  Margot, who uses academic jargon deliberately and is mocked for it. No "lowkey," "vibe check," "it's giving," "slay,"
  "rizz," "ghosting" (as a verb), "situationship." Era slang is allowed and sparing: "epic fail," "that's what she said,"
  "FML," "sexiled," "hella" (NorCal kids only — Kevin, Julian), "legit," "creeper," "facebook stalking," "the Twilight
  thing," "Team Edward." Jack says "wicked" never; he's from Ohio. Jack says "pop," not "soda." Lena says "y'all," "cher,"
  "making groceries," "neutral ground," "where y'at." Laney says "y'all" too, but Dallas, not New Orleans: "fixin' to."
- **Names:** Lena calls him "Costigan" when fighting, "Jack" when it matters, "Youngstown" when flirting, and once a
  season, "John." Jack calls her "Broussard," "Lena," and, rarely, "Magdalena" — which she allows only from him and only
  when he means it. Theo calls Jack "Costigan," "Father Jack," "my moral compass." People call Theo "Theodore" when he's
  in trouble.
- **Speeches are rare and earned.** One real speech per episode, maximum, usually in the duel or the climax.

\### 4.4 How heat is written: restraint is the fire
- The heat is in the **near**: the inch, the almost, the hand that does not move, the breath on a neck in a crowded
  room, the door that stays shut. Proximity, not contact. Specific bodies, specific details: the freckle at the hinge of
  her jaw, the cold of the medal against her collarbone, the way she pushes her sleeves up when she's about to win, the
  stubble he hasn't dealt with, the smell of smoke he can't get out of his jacket.
- **Withholding is active.** When Jack doesn't kiss her, the prose must make the not-kissing an event: show the cost.
- **Desire is good.** Never write desire as dirty. The Catholic claim is that desire is so good it's worth ordering. Jack
  wants her — the prose says so plainly. The restraint is a choice made by a man who wants, not a man who doesn't.
- **When sex is on the page** (other characters, other couples, and later possibly Jack and Lena per player choices):
  frank, literary, adult, specific to the people. Sensation and emotion, the comedy of bodies, the vulnerability, the
  morning after, the loneliness of the wrong bed. Never pornographic mechanics. Never glamorized and never punished by
  the narration; consequences come from character, not from the author's thumb.
- Everyone in sexual content is **18 or older** (see §9 birthdays).
- **HARD RULE (brief): Jack and Lena sleep only with each other, ever.** Neither has slept with anyone before the series,
  and neither sleeps with anyone else during it: not Wes, not Becca, not Skye, not Julian, not a
  stranger at a party. Their first time, whenever and however the player's choices bring it, is with each other. With
  other people the ceiling is **kissing, making out, real physical temptation and the near-miss** — charged, on the page,
  costing resolve — and then the line holds: an interruption, her stop, his stop, a door, a phone, a confession of the
  wrong name. "The loneliness of the wrong bed" belongs to the ensemble, never to Jack or Lena. Jealousy-as-POV may let
  Jack *fear* (a door closing, her coming home late in someone's jacket); the narration must never state or imply as fact
  that Lena slept with anyone, and the fear is answered on the page once they're together (S2E19: "Never. Not with anyone." — both of them).

\### 4.5 How faith is written: true, and hard
- **Flannery O'Connor, not a pamphlet.** Grace is real and it is violent, inconvenient, funny, and usually arrives
  through the wrong person. Sin has weight; so does mercy.
- **The sacraments work.** Confession actually absolves. The Eucharist is actually Christ. The narration never winks.
  But the narration never *proves* it either: Jack experiences grace the way people do — as peace he can't account for,
  as a sentence that arrives when he needed it, as the strength to not do the thing.
- **Prayer on the page:** short, real, unpolished. Jack prays like a man talking to someone in the next room. He uses the
  Hail Mary when he can't think, the Jesus Prayer when he's scared, and on bad nights the St. Michael prayer like a
  bouncer's checklist. Lena prays the Memorare and argues with God out loud.
- **Liturgy is accurate:** the real readings for the real Sunday, the real feast days, the real colors. Homilies are
  excerpted, never transcribed whole: two or three sentences that land.
- **Catholics are not the good guys.** Newman has hypocrites, bores, saints, fools, a gossip, a missionary who means it
  and a missionary who's faking it. The faith is true; the faithful are people.
- **The critics get their best arguments.** Margot is often right. Hale is often right. Julian is often the most
  Christ-like person in the room and doesn't believe a word of it. The story never sneers at belief or unbelief.

\### 4.6 How the arguments are written
- **Both brilliant, both real.** Real philosophy, real texts, real citations: Aquinas (*ST* I-II q.94 on natural law,
  q.109–114 on grace; *Summa contra Gentiles*), Augustine (*Confessions* VIII, *City of God* XIV, *Enchiridion*,
  "Dilige, et quod vis fac" — *Tractates on 1 John* 7.8), Pascal (*Pensées*, the wager, "cela vous abêtira"), Aristotle
  (*NE* II on habituation), Kierkegaard (*Attack upon "Christendom"*), Newman (*Grammar of Assent*, *Apologia*),
  Anscombe ("Modern Moral Philosophy"), MacIntyre (*After Virtue*), Bernard Williams ("Moral Luck," *Ethics and the
  Limits of Philosophy*), Nietzsche (*Genealogy*), Hume (*Dialogues*), Russell, Mackie (*The Miracle of Theism*), Dennett,
  Hitchens (*god is not Great*, 2007), Simone de Beauvoir (*The Second Sex*), Judith Butler, bell hooks, Mary Daly,
  Dorothy Day, Chesterton (*Orthodoxy*, *The Everlasting Man*), C.S. Lewis, Lewis vs. Tolkien on allegory, O'Connor
  (*Mystery and Manners*, "A Good Man Is Hard to Find," "Revelation," "Parker's Back"), Waugh (*Brideshead*), Greene
  (*The Power and the Glory*, *The End of the Affair*), Percy (*The Moviegoer*, *Love in the Ruins*), Dostoevsky (the
  Grand Inquisitor; Zosima), Weil, Benedict XVI (*Deus Caritas Est*, *Spe Salvi*, *Caritas in Veritate* — July 7, 2009),
  John Paul II (*Theology of the Body*, *Veritatis Splendor*, *Evangelium Vitae*).
- **Quote accurately or paraphrase honestly.** If a writer isn't sure of a quote, paraphrase it in-character ("Pascal says
  something like…").
- **Lena wins about as often as Jack does.** Across a season, tally. Lena's default mode is Thomist-with-a-machete: she
  trusts reason, nature, the body, form, the visible Church; she is merciful toward persons and merciless toward
  arguments. Jack's default mode is Augustinian-with-a-smoker's-cough: he trusts grace, distrusts nature (his own
  especially), feels the fall in his bones, is merciful toward arguments and hard on himself. **They swap positions when
  the facts get personal** — that's where the best duels live.
- **The arguments are flirtation and also real.** Every duel has a moment where it stops being a game. Winning isn't
  always good. Conceding the right point at the right moment is the most romantic thing either of them can do.
- **Politics (reconciled with the brief's map after S1E01):** both are *nearly* politically homeless, both deeply
  pro-life, both convinced that both parties betray the Church, and each thinks the other is wrong for leaning where they
  lean. **Lena** is a Catholic Worker type (Dorothy Day), a pro-life Louisiana Democrat in her bones — union fishermen,
  Charity Hospital, the levees, the death penalty (against), immigrants (for), Katrina as proof the state abandons the
  poor — and ferocious about abortion and euthanasia. **Jack** is more comfortable on the right: a law-and-order Rust Belt
  conservative raised in a Kennedy-Democrat union house (Frank) who broke with it at the kitchen table. Labor is still in
  his blood (his father's layoff is personal) and he distrusts the market's mercy as much as the government's, but his
  instincts run to order, crime and punishment and the culture war, and he votes like it. 🔒 The irony the show never
  states: the law-and-order boy is the reason his brother is in prison. **Over the run (brief):** Lena's struggle deepens
  as the Democrats harden on abortion and as the LGBT movement shifts, in her view, from human dignity to remaking sex and
  the family; Jack keeps hearing racial overtones inside what he considers reasonable disagreements with Obama; the Trump
  years are very hard on Jack (immigration enforcement turning, as he sees it, into dehumanization); Dobbs (2022) is joy
  for both, then ash (mailed pills backed by Republicans too, IVF's expansion); both grow disgusted with the whole system.
  These are convictions written from inside; Margot, Theo, Hale, Kevin, Julian and Yusuf get their best arguments. They
  fight about the health-care bill and Stupak, Notre Dame and Obama (May 2009), Ted Kennedy's funeral, the death penalty,
  immigration, Traficant (Jack: a crook; Frank: *our* crook), and whether the bishops are cowards or courageous about the
  abuse scandal (they agree the bishops failed; they disagree about what that means for obedience).

\### 4.7 How comedy works
- **Character comedy, never mockery.** The funniest people are funny on purpose (Lena, Theo, Laney, Margot) and the
  second-funniest are funny by accident (Priya's laminated notes, Joey Abruzzo's volume, Doug Pruitt existing).
- **Running jokes pay off** (see Part IV §26). The scar story. The cigarette reliquary. Margot's whiteboard. The 3 a.m.
  fire alarm. The Anchor's pie ratings.
- **Banter has rhythm**: set, turn, topper — then break the pattern; let someone fail to land one.
- **Jack is funny in a way that keeps people at arm's length.** The show eventually notices that.
- **The sacred is never the butt of the joke**; Catholics are, often.

\### 4.8 Content standards (per the brief)
- Sex, desire, bodies, drug use, violence, addiction, overdose, death and grief: frank and literary, HBO-level, always
  in service of character and emotion. Morning-afters, bad sex, good sex, funny sex, the loneliness of a hookup. Never
  pornographic mechanics.
- **Everyone in any sexual content is 18+.** Every freshman in the show is 18 or older at move-in (see §9).
- **Sexual assault:** may be a storyline; if used, it is centered on the survivor's experience and aftermath, never
  titillating, never a twist. **Currently no assault storyline is scheduled.** If one is introduced, it requires a
  Showrunner note and a revision-log entry. **HARD RULE (brief): never in Jack's or Lena's backgrounds, and never
  happening to either of them** — not as a wound, not as the cause of a wound, not as the event behind a secret. Their
  wounds are family rupture, grief, violence, crime, illness, addiction in the family, non-sexual harm, faith crises.
- **HARD RULE (brief): Jack and Lena sleep only with each other** (see §4.4). Applies to backstory and to every season.
- Addiction and overdose: real, unglamorous. Someone may not make it (see §19, Death register).
- **Hard lines:** the narrative never mocks the faith. No cheap villains, no secular strawmen. **Jack and Lena never
  die.**
- Real people (politicians, clergy, journalists, celebrities) appear only through the news, TV, the radio, the paper,
  and characters' opinions about public events. Never invent private conduct for a real person. Never assert, in-world,
  any fact about a real private event that the public record does not support.
